I might not be actively thinking about the manager right that second, but that tight, wound-up feeling is still just... there. \n\nIt's like I can't fully relax. My mind always drifts back to what happened on Friday, or what I'm going to have to deal with on Monday. So, no, it doesn't really go away, even when I'm away from the building.\n\nDoctor: Beyond this situation with your manager and the procedures at work, are there other things in your life right now — like your health, finances, relationships, or other responsibilities — that are causing you to worry or feel anxious?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's really just that one thing causing all of this.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf this situation with your manager were to be completely resolved — say he left the company or the procedures went back to how they were — do you think this wound-up, anxious feeling would go away, or do you think it might stick around even after the problem itself is solved?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I guess I just haven't paid attention to whether the feeling comes up when I'm not focused on the manager or the inspection line. I just know I'm lying there feeling on edge, but I couldn't tell you for sure what exactly is going through my head every single time it happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That's when the trouble sleeping and being so wound up at home really became obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these moments of feeling wound up and anxious, do they tend to happen when you're thinking about work or dealing with work-related situations, or do you find yourself feeling this way even when you're not focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely tied to work. I mean, the situation hasn't ended. I still have to report to him every day and deal with all the procedures he changed. I haven't had any time where the problem was solved and I just kept feeling this way for months on my own. I might not be actively thinking about the manager right that second, but that tight, wound-up feeling is still just... there. \n\nIt's like I can't fully relax. My mind always drifts back to what happened on Friday, or what I'm going to have to deal with on Monday. So, no, it doesn't really go away, even when I'm away from the building.\n\nDoctor: Beyond this situation with your manager and the procedures at work, are there other things in your life right now — like your health, finances, relationships, or other responsibilities — that are causing you to worry or feel anxious?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... everything else is actually fine. My husband and I are doing good, my health is fine, and we don't have any major money issues or anything like that. \n\nIt really is just... just work. If it weren't for this situation with the new manager and all the changes on the inspection line, I wouldn't be feeling this way at all. It's really just that one thing causing all of this.\n\nDoctor: If this situation with your manager were to be completely resolved — say he left the company or the procedures went back to how they were — do you think this wound-up, anxious feeling would go away, or do you think it might stick around even after the problem itself is solved?\n\nPatient: I really think it would go away. I mean, like I said before, this situation hasn't ended yet. I still have to go in there and report to him, and I'm still dealing with all those new procedures every single day. So I haven't had a chance to see what it would be like if it were fixed. But I really believe that if he were gone, or things went back to normal... I'd be fine. I haven't had any time where the problem was solved and I just kept feeling this way for months on my own. It's just because I'm still in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: Since this started two months ago, has the intensity of that wound-up, anxious feeling stayed pretty much the same the whole time, or has it gotten worse, or has it actually improved at all even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's stayed pretty much the same. I mean, it definitely hasn't gotten any better, because the situation at work hasn't changed at all. But I don't know that it's gotten a whole lot worse, either. \n\nIt's just... kind of a constant, steady level of being on edge. Ever since he came in and changed all the procedures a couple months ago, that feeling has just been there, pretty much at the exact same intensity the whole time.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed at night unable to sleep, or when you're feeling that wound-up sensation — are you actively thinking about work and the manager in those moments, or does the anxious feeling come up even when your mind isn't focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I know I think about work a lot when I'm lying there in bed... but I haven't really noticed if I feel that way when my mind is on other things. I guess I just haven't paid attention to whether the feeling comes up when I'm not focused on the manager or the inspection line. I just know I'm lying there feeling on edge, but I couldn't tell you for sure what exactly is going through my head every single time it happens.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask this a different way: If you were to take a full week of vacation away from work — completely away from the office, your manager, and everything related to it — do you think that wound-up, anxious feeling would go away during that time, or would it still be there with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't taken a vacation since all this started, so I haven't had a chance to see how I'd feel. I mean, I'd like to think I'd feel better if I were away from it all for a week, but honestly, I just don't know for certain if that feeling would completely go away or if it would kind of... follow me, you know? I just haven't been in that situation to find out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's like everything I do is suddenly wrong. \n\nAnd the problem is, I can't seem to leave work at work anymore. I come home just completely wound up. I end up snapping at my husband over nothing, and then I lie awake at night just replaying all these meetings in my head. It's exhausting. I just don't feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of being wound up and having trouble sleeping — was it around the time this new manager arrived, or had you been feeling this way before that?\n\nPatient: I was doing completely fine before he got there. None of this was happening before the new operations manager came in. \n\nThe trouble really started building within just a few weeks of him making all those changes to our procedures. I'd say... by the second month, I knew for sure that my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. That's when the trouble sleeping and being so wound up at home really became obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these moments of feeling wound up and anxious, do they tend to happen when you're thinking about work or dealing with work-related situations, or do you find yourself feeling this way even when you're not focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely tied to work. I mean, the situation hasn't ended. I still have to report to him every day and deal with all the procedures he changed. It's not like the issue was resolved and I just kept feeling this way for months afterward for no reason. It's happening because I'm still right in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: On days when you don't have to go to work, or when you're doing something completely unrelated to this manager or the procedures — like spending time with your husband or doing something you enjoy — do you still find yourself feeling that wound-up, anxious feeling, or does it mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it doesn't completely go away on the weekends, no. I wish it did. Even when I'm just sitting on the couch with my husband trying to watch a movie, or if we go out to run errands... I might not be actively thinking about the manager right that second, but that tight, wound-up feeling is still just... there. \n\nIt's like I can't fully relax. My mind always drifts back to what happened on Friday, or what I'm going to have to deal with on Monday. So, no, it doesn't really go away, even when I'm away from the building.\n\nDoctor: Beyond this situation with your manager and the procedures at work, are there other things in your life right now — like your health, finances, relationships, or other responsibilities — that are causing you to worry or feel anxious?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, like I said before, this situation hasn't ended yet. I still have to go in there and report to him, and I'm still dealing with all those new procedures every single day. So I haven't had a chance to see what it would be like if it were fixed. But I really believe that if he were gone, or things went back to normal... I'd be fine.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.21\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist here. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... things have just been really difficult at work lately. We got a new operations manager, and he's just been picking apart the inspection procedures I've used for years. It's like everything I do is suddenly wrong. \n\nAnd the problem is, I can't seem to leave work at work anymore. I come home just completely wound up. I end up snapping at my husband over nothing, and then I lie awake at night just replaying all these meetings in my head. It's exhausting. And then the next day, I'm second-guessing routine quality calls that I used to make without even thinking twice about it. I just don't feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of being wound up and having trouble sleeping — was it around the time this new manager arrived, or had you been feeling this way before that?\n\nPatient: I was doing completely fine before he got there. None of this was happening before the new operations manager came in. \n\nThe trouble really started building within just a few weeks of him making all those changes to our procedures. I'd say... by the second month, I knew for sure that my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. That's when the trouble sleeping and being so wound up at home really became obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these moments of feeling wound up and anxious, do they tend to happen when you're thinking about work or dealing with work-related situations, or do you find yourself feeling this way even when you're not focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely tied to work. I mean, the situation hasn't ended. I still have to report to him every day and deal with all the procedures he changed. It's not like the issue was resolved and I just kept feeling this way for months afterward for no reason. It's happening because I'm still right in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: On days when you don't have to go to work, or when you're doing something completely unrelated to this manager or the procedures — like spending time with your husband or doing something you enjoy — do you still find yourself feeling that wound-up, anxious feeling, or does it mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it doesn't completely go away on the weekends, no. I wish it did. Even when I'm just sitting on the couch with my husband trying to watch a movie, or if we go out to run errands... I might not be actively thinking about the manager right that second, but that tight, wound-up feeling is still just... there. \n\nIt's like I can't fully relax. My mind always drifts back to what happened on Friday, or what I'm going to have to deal with on Monday. So, no, it doesn't really go away, even when I'm away from the building.\n\nDoctor: Beyond this situation with your manager and the procedures at work, are there other things in your life right now — like your health, finances, relationships, or other responsibilities — that are causing you to worry or feel anxious?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... everything else is actually fine. My husband and I are doing good, my health is fine, and we don't have any major money issues or anything like that. \n\nIt really is just... just work. If it weren't for this situation with the new manager and all the changes on the inspection line, I wouldn't be feeling this way at all. It's really just that one thing causing all of this.\n\nDoctor: If this situation with your manager were to be completely resolved — say he left the company or the procedures went back to how they were — do you think this wound-up, anxious feeling would go away, or do you think it might stick around even after the problem itself is solved?\n\nPatient: I really think it would go away. I mean, like I said before, this situation hasn't ended yet. I still have to go in there and report to him, and I'm still dealing with all those new procedures every single day. So I haven't had a chance to see what it would be like if it were fixed. But I really believe that if he were gone, or things went back to normal... I'd be fine. I haven't had any time where the problem was solved and I just kept feeling this way for months on my own. It's just because I'm still in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: Since this started two months ago, has the intensity of that wound-up, anxious feeling stayed pretty much the same the whole time, or has it gotten worse, or has it actually improved at all even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's stayed pretty much the same. I mean, it definitely hasn't gotten any better, because the situation at work hasn't changed at all. But I don't know that it's gotten a whole lot worse, either. \n\nIt's just... kind of a constant, steady level of being on edge. Ever since he came in and changed all the procedures a couple months ago, that feeling has just been there, pretty much at the exact same intensity the whole time.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed at night unable to sleep, or when you're feeling that wound-up sensation — are you actively thinking about work and the manager in those moments, or does the anxious feeling come up even when your mind isn't focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I know I think about work a lot when I'm lying there in bed... but I haven't really noticed if I feel that way when my mind is on other things. I guess I just haven't paid attention to whether the feeling comes up when I'm not focused on the manager or the inspection line. I just know I'm lying there feeling on edge, but I couldn't tell you for sure what exactly is going through my head every single time it happens.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask this a different way: If you were to take a full week of vacation away from work — completely away from the office, your manager, and everything related to it — do you think that wound-up, anxious feeling would go away during that time, or would it still be there with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't taken a vacation since all this started, so I haven't had a chance to see how I'd feel. I mean, I'd like to think I'd feel better if I were away from it all for a week, but honestly, I just don't know for certain if that feeling would completely go away or if it would kind of... follow me, you know? I just haven't been in that situation to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that really captures your full attention — like reading a book you can't put down, or having an engaging conversation with your husband — does that wound-up, anxious feeling fade into the background while you're focused on that activity, or does it stay with you no matter what you're doing?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm really not sure about that, either. I haven't paid close attention to whether it fades or not. \n\nIt feels like it's always there, but... I guess if I'm really focused on something else, maybe I'm just not noticing it for a minute? I don't really know. I haven't been reading much lately, and when I talk to my husband, we usually end up talking about my manager anyway. So I just couldn't say for sure if the feeling actually goes away when I'm distracted.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you noticed any days or periods where you felt that wound-up, on-edge feeling was noticeably less intense or actually absent — even if just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't kept track of it like that. It just feels like it's been a constant thing since my manager started getting on my case, but I haven't really stopped to think if there was a specific day or a few hours where it was less intense. I just know I feel wound up most of the time.\n\nDoctor: When you're away from work and something happens that completely distracts you — like if your husband made you laugh really hard, or you got unexpected good news — does that wound-up feeling actually decrease in those moments, or does it stay at the same level no matter what?\n\nPatient: I don't think I can really say. I haven't really had any unexpected good news lately, and like I said, when I'm with my husband, we're usually just talking about work. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether a laugh makes it go away for a second. It just feels like that tension is always kind of sitting there, you know? I haven't really noticed it dropping off in those kinds of moments.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.21\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist here. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... things have just been really difficult at work lately. We got a new operations manager, and he's just been picking apart the inspection procedures I've used for years. It's like everything I do is suddenly wrong. \n\nAnd the problem is, I can't seem to leave work at work anymore. I come home just completely wound up. I end up snapping at my husband over nothing, and then I lie awake at night just replaying all these meetings in my head. It's exhausting. And then the next day, I'm second-guessing routine quality calls that I used to make without even thinking twice about it. I just don't feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of being wound up and having trouble sleeping — was it around the time this new manager arrived, or had you been feeling this way before that?\n\nPatient: I was doing completely fine before he got there. None of this was happening before the new operations manager came in. \n\nThe trouble really started building within just a few weeks of him making all those changes to our procedures. I'd say... by the second month, I knew for sure that my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. That's when the trouble sleeping and being so wound up at home really became obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these moments of feeling wound up and anxious, do they tend to happen when you're thinking about work or dealing with work-related situations, or do you find yourself feeling this way even when you're not focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely tied to work. I mean, the situation hasn't ended. I still have to report to him every day and deal with all the procedures he changed. It's not like the issue was resolved and I just kept feeling this way for months afterward for no reason. It's happening because I'm still right in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: On days when you don't have to go to work, or when you're doing something completely unrelated to this manager or the procedures — like spending time with your husband or doing something you enjoy — do you still find yourself feeling that wound-up, anxious feeling, or does it mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it doesn't completely go away on the weekends, no. I wish it did. Even when I'm just sitting on the couch with my husband trying to watch a movie, or if we go out to run errands... I might not be actively thinking about the manager right that second, but that tight, wound-up feeling is still just... there. \n\nIt's like I can't fully relax. My mind always drifts back to what happened on Friday, or what I'm going to have to deal with on Monday. So, no, it doesn't really go away, even when I'm away from the building.\n\nDoctor: Beyond this situation with your manager and the procedures at work, are there other things in your life right now — like your health, finances, relationships, or other responsibilities — that are causing you to worry or feel anxious?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... everything else is actually fine. My husband and I are doing good, my health is fine, and we don't have any major money issues or anything like that. \n\nIt really is just... just work. If it weren't for this situation with the new manager and all the changes on the inspection line, I wouldn't be feeling this way at all. It's really just that one thing causing all of this.\n\nDoctor: If this situation with your manager were to be completely resolved — say he left the company or the procedures went back to how they were — do you think this wound-up, anxious feeling would go away, or do you think it might stick around even after the problem itself is solved?\n\nPatient: I really think it would go away. I mean, like I said before, this situation hasn't ended yet. I still have to go in there and report to him, and I'm still dealing with all those new procedures every single day. So I haven't had a chance to see what it would be like if it were fixed. But I really believe that if he were gone, or things went back to normal... I'd be fine. I haven't had any time where the problem was solved and I just kept feeling this way for months on my own. It's just because I'm still in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: Since this started two months ago, has the intensity of that wound-up, anxious feeling stayed pretty much the same the whole time, or has it gotten worse, or has it actually improved at all even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's stayed pretty much the same. I mean, it definitely hasn't gotten any better, because the situation at work hasn't changed at all. But I don't know that it's gotten a whole lot worse, either. \n\nIt's just... kind of a constant, steady level of being on edge. Ever since he came in and changed all the procedures a couple months ago, that feeling has just been there, pretty much at the exact same intensity the whole time.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed at night unable to sleep, or when you're feeling that wound-up sensation — are you actively thinking about work and the manager in those moments, or does the anxious feeling come up even when your mind isn't focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I know I think about work a lot when I'm lying there in bed... but I haven't really noticed if I feel that way when my mind is on other things. I guess I just haven't paid attention to whether the feeling comes up when I'm not focused on the manager or the inspection line. I just know I'm lying there feeling on edge, but I couldn't tell you for sure what exactly is going through my head every single time it happens.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask this a different way: If you were to take a full week of vacation away from work — completely away from the office, your manager, and everything related to it — do you think that wound-up, anxious feeling would go away during that time, or would it still be there with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't taken a vacation since all this started, so I haven't had a chance to see how I'd feel. I mean, I'd like to think I'd feel better if I were away from it all for a week, but honestly, I just don't know for certain if that feeling would completely go away or if it would kind of... follow me, you know? I just haven't been in that situation to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that really captures your full attention — like reading a book you can't put down, or having an engaging conversation with your husband — does that wound-up, anxious feeling fade into the background while you're focused on that activity, or does it stay with you no matter what you're doing?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm really not sure about that, either. I haven't paid close attention to whether it fades or not. \n\nIt feels like it's always there, but... I guess if I'm really focused on something else, maybe I'm just not noticing it for a minute? I don't really know. I haven't been reading much lately, and when I talk to my husband, we usually end up talking about my manager anyway. So I just couldn't say for sure if the feeling actually goes away when I'm distracted.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you noticed any days or periods where you felt that wound-up, on-edge feeling was noticeably less intense or actually absent — even if just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't kept track of it like that. It just feels like it's been a constant thing since my manager started getting on my case, but I haven't really stopped to think if there was a specific day or a few hours where it was less intense. I just know I feel wound up most of the time.\n\nDoctor: When you're away from work and something happens that completely distracts you — like if your husband made you laugh really hard, or you got unexpected good news — does that wound-up feeling actually decrease in those moments, or does it stay at the same level no matter what?\n\nPatient: I don't think I can really say. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist here. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... things have just been really difficult at work lately. We got a new operations manager, and he's just been picking apart the inspection procedures I've used for years. It's like everything I do is suddenly wrong. \n\nAnd the problem is, I can't seem to leave work at work anymore. I come home just completely wound up. I end up snapping at my husband over nothing, and then I lie awake at night just replaying all these meetings in my head. It's exhausting. And then the next day, I'm second-guessing routine quality calls that I used to make without even thinking twice about it. I just don't feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of being wound up and having trouble sleeping — was it around the time this new manager arrived, or had you been feeling this way before that?\n\nPatient: I was doing completely fine before he got there. None of this was happening before the new operations manager came in. \n\nThe trouble really started building within just a few weeks of him making all those changes to our procedures. I'd say... by the second month, I knew for sure that my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. That's when the trouble sleeping and being so wound up at home really became obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these moments of feeling wound up and anxious, do they tend to happen when you're thinking about work or dealing with work-related situations, or do you find yourself feeling this way even when you're not focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely tied to work. I mean, the situation hasn't ended. I still have to report to him every day and deal with all the procedures he changed. It's not like the issue was resolved and I just kept feeling this way for months afterward for no reason. It's happening because I'm still right in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: On days when you don't have to go to work, or when you're doing something completely unrelated to this manager or the procedures — like spending time with your husband or doing something you enjoy — do you still find yourself feeling that wound-up, anxious feeling, or does it mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it doesn't completely go away on the weekends, no. I wish it did. Even when I'm just sitting on the couch with my husband trying to watch a movie, or if we go out to run errands... I might not be actively thinking about the manager right that second, but that tight, wound-up feeling is still just... there. \n\nIt's like I can't fully relax. My mind always drifts back to what happened on Friday, or what I'm going to have to deal with on Monday. So, no, it doesn't really go away, even when I'm away from the building.\n\nDoctor: Beyond this situation with your manager and the procedures at work, are there other things in your life right now — like your health, finances, relationships, or other responsibilities — that are causing you to worry or feel anxious?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... everything else is actually fine. My husband and I are doing good, my health is fine, and we don't have any major money issues or anything like that. \n\nIt really is just... just work. If it weren't for this situation with the new manager and all the changes on the inspection line, I wouldn't be feeling this way at all. It's really just that one thing causing all of this.\n\nDoctor: If this situation with your manager were to be completely resolved — say he left the company or the procedures went back to how they were — do you think this wound-up, anxious feeling would go away, or do you think it might stick around even after the problem itself is solved?\n\nPatient: I really think it would go away. I mean, like I said before, this situation hasn't ended yet. I still have to go in there and report to him, and I'm still dealing with all those new procedures every single day. So I haven't had a chance to see what it would be like if it were fixed. But I really believe that if he were gone, or things went back to normal... I'd be fine. I haven't had any time where the problem was solved and I just kept feeling this way for months on my own. It's just because I'm still in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: Since this started two months ago, has the intensity of that wound-up, anxious feeling stayed pretty much the same the whole time, or has it gotten worse, or has it actually improved at all even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's stayed pretty much the same. I mean, it definitely hasn't gotten any better, because the situation at work hasn't changed at all. But I don't know that it's gotten a whole lot worse, either. \n\nIt's just... kind of a constant, steady level of being on edge. Ever since he came in and changed all the procedures a couple months ago, that feeling has just been there, pretty much at the exact same intensity the whole time.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed at night unable to sleep, or when you're feeling that wound-up sensation — are you actively thinking about work and the manager in those moments, or does the anxious feeling come up even when your mind isn't focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I know I think about work a lot when I'm lying there in bed... but I haven't really noticed if I feel that way when my mind is on other things. I guess I just haven't paid attention to whether the feeling comes up when I'm not focused on the manager or the inspection line. I just know I'm lying there feeling on edge, but I couldn't tell you for sure what exactly is going through my head every single time it happens.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask this a different way: If you were to take a full week of vacation away from work — completely away from the office, your manager, and everything related to it — do you think that wound-up, anxious feeling would go away during that time, or would it still be there with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't taken a vacation since all this started, so I haven't had a chance to see how I'd feel. I mean, I'd like to think I'd feel better if I were away from it all for a week, but honestly, I just don't know for certain if that feeling would completely go away or if it would kind of... follow me, you know? I just haven't been in that situation to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that really captures your full attention — like reading a book you can't put down, or having an engaging conversation with your husband — does that wound-up, anxious feeling fade into the background while you're focused on that activity, or does it stay with you no matter what you're doing?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm really not sure about that, either. I haven't paid close attention to whether it fades or not. \n\nIt feels like it's always there, but... I guess if I'm really focused on something else, maybe I'm just not noticing it for a minute? I don't really know. I haven't been reading much lately, and when I talk to my husband, we usually end up talking about my manager anyway. So I just couldn't say for sure if the feeling actually goes away when I'm distracted.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you noticed any days or periods where you felt that wound-up, on-edge feeling was noticeably less intense or actually absent — even if just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't kept track of it like that. I just haven't been in that situation to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that really captures your full attention — like reading a book you can't put down, or having an engaging conversation with your husband — does that wound-up, anxious feeling fade into the background while you're focused on that activity, or does it stay with you no matter what you're doing?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm really not sure about that, either. I haven't paid close attention to whether it fades or not. \n\nIt feels like it's always there, but... I guess if I'm really focused on something else, maybe I'm just not noticing it for a minute? I don't really know. I haven't been reading much lately, and when I talk to my husband, we usually end up talking about my manager anyway. So I just couldn't say for sure if the feeling actually goes away when I'm distracted.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you noticed any days or periods where you felt that wound-up, on-edge feeling was noticeably less intense or actually absent — even if just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't kept track of it like that. It just feels like it's been a constant thing since my manager started getting on my case, but I haven't really stopped to think if there was a specific day or a few hours where it was less intense. I just know I feel wound up most of the time.\n\nDoctor: When you're away from work and something happens that completely distracts you — like if your husband made you laugh really hard, or you got unexpected good news — does that wound-up feeling actually decrease in those moments, or does it stay at the same level no matter what?\n\nPatient: I don't think I can really say. I haven't really had any unexpected good news lately, and like I said, when I'm with my husband, we're usually just talking about work. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether a laugh makes it go away for a second. It just feels like that tension is always kind of sitting there, you know? I haven't really noticed it dropping off in those kinds of moments.\n\nDoctor: I know you haven't taken a vacation since this started, but let me ask: on weekends or evenings when you're completely away from work and the manager, do you find that the wound-up feeling gradually gets better as the hours pass, or does it stay pretty much the same level of intensity throughout your time off?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that, to be honest. I haven't paid attention to whether it actually fades as the weekend goes on. It feels like I'm just always thinking about having to go back in on Monday, so I can't say for certain if the intensity drops on a Saturday night or if it just stays exactly the same. I just haven't tracked it that closely to know if it gradually gets better.\n\nDoctor: If your manager were fired tomorrow and the procedures went back to exactly how they were before, but you still had to keep working at the same job in the same building — do you think that wound-up feeling would go away, or do you think it might stay with you even though the stressor itself would be gone?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably go away. I mean, the situation hasn't ended—he's still my manager, and I still have to do things his way. So I haven't really had a chance to see what would happen if the problem was actually fixed. But I feel like if he was gone and things went back to normal, I wouldn't feel this way anymore. It's not like I've had these issues just keep going on for months after a problem was already resolved. It's really just tied to him and the changes he made.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist here. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... things have just been really difficult at work lately. We got a new operations manager, and he's just been picking apart the inspection procedures I've used for years. It's like everything I do is suddenly wrong. \n\nAnd the problem is, I can't seem to leave work at work anymore. I come home just completely wound up. I end up snapping at my husband over nothing, and then I lie awake at night just replaying all these meetings in my head. It's exhausting. And then the next day, I'm second-guessing routine quality calls that I used to make without even thinking twice about it. I just don't feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of being wound up and having trouble sleeping — was it around the time this new manager arrived, or had you been feeling this way before that?\n\nPatient: I was doing completely fine before he got there. None of this was happening before the new operations manager came in. \n\nThe trouble really started building within just a few weeks of him making all those changes to our procedures. I'd say... by the second month, I knew for sure that my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. That's when the trouble sleeping and being so wound up at home really became obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these moments of feeling wound up and anxious, do they tend to happen when you're thinking about work or dealing with work-related situations, or do you find yourself feeling this way even when you're not focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely tied to work. I mean, the situation hasn't ended. I still have to report to him every day and deal with all the procedures he changed. It's not like the issue was resolved and I just kept feeling this way for months afterward for no reason. It's happening because I'm still right in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: On days when you don't have to go to work, or when you're doing something completely unrelated to this manager or the procedures — like spending time with your husband or doing something you enjoy — do you still find yourself feeling that wound-up, anxious feeling, or does it mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it doesn't completely go away on the weekends, no. I wish it did. Even when I'm just sitting on the couch with my husband trying to watch a movie, or if we go out to run errands... I might not be actively thinking about the manager right that second, but that tight, wound-up feeling is still just... there. \n\nIt's like I can't fully relax. My mind always drifts back to what happened on Friday, or what I'm going to have to deal with on Monday. So, no, it doesn't really go away, even when I'm away from the building.\n\nDoctor: Beyond this situation with your manager and the procedures at work, are there other things in your life right now — like your health, finances, relationships, or other responsibilities — that are causing you to worry or feel anxious?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... everything else is actually fine. My husband and I are doing good, my health is fine, and we don't have any major money issues or anything like that. \n\nIt really is just... just work. If it weren't for this situation with the new manager and all the changes on the inspection line, I wouldn't be feeling this way at all. It's really just that one thing causing all of this.\n\nDoctor: If this situation with your manager were to be completely resolved — say he left the company or the procedures went back to how they were — do you think this wound-up, anxious feeling would go away, or do you think it might stick around even after the problem itself is solved?\n\nPatient: I really think it would go away. I mean, like I said before, this situation hasn't ended yet. I still have to go in there and report to him, and I'm still dealing with all those new procedures every single day. So I haven't had a chance to see what it would be like if it were fixed. But I really believe that if he were gone, or things went back to normal... I'd be fine. I haven't had any time where the problem was solved and I just kept feeling this way for months on my own. I mean... everything else is actually fine. My husband and I are doing good, my health is fine, and we don't have any major money issues or anything like that. \n\nIt really is just... just work. If it weren't for this situation with the new manager and all the changes on the inspection line, I wouldn't be feeling this way at all. It's really just that one thing causing all of this.\n\nDoctor: If this situation with your manager were to be completely resolved — say he left the company or the procedures went back to how they were — do you think this wound-up, anxious feeling would go away, or do you think it might stick around even after the problem itself is solved?\n\nPatient: I really think it would go away. I mean, like I said before, this situation hasn't ended yet. I still have to go in there and report to him, and I'm still dealing with all those new procedures every single day. So I haven't had a chance to see what it would be like if it were fixed. But I really believe that if he were gone, or things went back to normal... I'd be fine. I haven't had any time where the problem was solved and I just kept feeling this way for months on my own. It's just because I'm still in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: Since this started two months ago, has the intensity of that wound-up, anxious feeling stayed pretty much the same the whole time, or has it gotten worse, or has it actually improved at all even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's stayed pretty much the same. I mean, it definitely hasn't gotten any better, because the situation at work hasn't changed at all. But I don't know that it's gotten a whole lot worse, either. \n\nIt's just... kind of a constant, steady level of being on edge. Ever since he came in and changed all the procedures a couple months ago, that feeling has just been there, pretty much at the exact same intensity the whole time.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed at night unable to sleep, or when you're feeling that wound-up sensation — are you actively thinking about work and the manager in those moments, or does the anxious feeling come up even when your mind isn't focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I know I think about work a lot when I'm lying there in bed... but I haven't really noticed if I feel that way when my mind is on other things. I guess I just haven't paid attention to whether the feeling comes up when I'm not focused on the manager or the inspection line. I just know I'm lying there feeling on edge, but I couldn't tell you for sure what exactly is going through my head every single time it happens.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask this a different way: If you were to take a full week of vacation away from work — completely away from the office, your manager, and everything related to it — do you think that wound-up, anxious feeling would go away during that time, or would it still be there with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't taken a vacation since all this started, so I haven't had a chance to see how I'd feel. I mean, I'd like to think I'd feel better if I were away from it all for a week, but honestly, I just don't know for certain if that feeling would completely go away or if it would kind of... follow me, you know? I just haven't been in that situation to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that really captures your full attention — like reading a book you can't put down, or having an engaging conversation with your husband — does that wound-up, anxious feeling fade into the background while you're focused on that activity, or does it stay with you no matter what you're doing?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm really not sure about that, either. I haven't paid close attention to whether it fades or not. \n\nIt feels like it's always there, but... I guess if I'm really focused on something else, maybe I'm just not noticing it for a minute? I don't really know. I haven't been reading much lately, and when I talk to my husband, we usually end up talking about my manager anyway. So I just couldn't say for sure if the feeling actually goes away when I'm distracted.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you noticed any days or periods where you felt that wound-up, on-edge feeling was noticeably less intense or actually absent — even if just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't kept track of it like that. It just feels like it's been a constant thing since my manager started getting on my case, but I haven't really stopped to think if there was a specific day or a few hours where it was less intense. I just know I feel wound up most of the time.\n\nDoctor: When you're away from work and something happens that completely distracts you — like if your husband made you laugh really hard, or you got unexpected good news — does that wound-up feeling actually decrease in those moments, or does it stay at the same level no matter what?\n\nPatient: I don't think I can really say. I haven't really had any unexpected good news lately, and like I said, when I'm with my husband, we're usually just talking about work. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether a laugh makes it go away for a second. It just feels like that tension is always kind of sitting there, you know? I haven't really noticed it dropping off in those kinds of moments.\n\nDoctor: I know you haven't taken a vacation since this started, but let me ask: on weekends or evenings when you're completely away from work and the manager, do you find that the wound-up feeling gradually gets better as the hours pass, or does it stay pretty much the same level of intensity throughout your time off?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that, to be honest. I haven't paid attention to whether it actually fades as the weekend goes on. It feels like I'm just always thinking about having to go back in on Monday, so I can't say for certain if the intensity drops on a Saturday night or if it just stays exactly the same. I just haven't tracked it that closely to know if it gradually gets better.\n\nDoctor: If your manager were fired tomorrow and the procedures went back to exactly how they were before, but you still had to keep working at the same job in the same building — do you think that wound-up feeling would go away, or do you think it might stay with you even though the stressor itself would be gone?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably go away. I mean, the situation hasn't ended—he's still my manager, and I still have to do things his way. So I haven't really had a chance to see what would happen if the problem was actually fixed. But I feel like if he was gone and things went back to normal, I wouldn't feel this way anymore. It's not like I've had these issues just keep going on for months after a problem was already resolved. It's really just tied to him and the changes he made.\n\nDoctor: Since this all started two months ago, have you ever had a full day or longer where you weren't thinking about work or your manager at all — and if so, did that wound-up, on-edge feeling actually go away during that time, or was it still there even when work wasn't on your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had a day like that. Work is always on my mind, even on the weekends. So I can't really say if the feeling would go away if I wasn't thinking about it, because I'm always thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I end up snapping at my husband over nothing, and then I lie awake at night just replaying all these meetings in my head. It's exhausting. And then the next day, I'm second-guessing routine quality calls that I used to make without even thinking twice about it. I just don't feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of being wound up and having trouble sleeping — was it around the time this new manager arrived, or had you been feeling this way before that?\n\nPatient: I was doing completely fine before he got there. None of this was happening before the new operations manager came in. \n\nThe trouble really started building within just a few weeks of him making all those changes to our procedures. I'd say... by the second month, I knew for sure that my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. That's when the trouble sleeping and being so wound up at home really became obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these moments of feeling wound up and anxious, do they tend to happen when you're thinking about work or dealing with work-related situations, or do you find yourself feeling this way even when you're not focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely tied to work. I mean, the situation hasn't ended. I still have to report to him every day and deal with all the procedures he changed. It's not like the issue was resolved and I just kept feeling this way for months afterward for no reason. It's happening because I'm still right in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: On days when you don't have to go to work, or when you're doing something completely unrelated to this manager or the procedures — like spending time with your husband or doing something you enjoy — do you still find yourself feeling that wound-up, anxious feeling, or does it mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it doesn't completely go away on the weekends, no. I wish it did. Even when I'm just sitting on the couch with my husband trying to watch a movie, or if we go out to run errands... I might not be actively thinking about the manager right that second, but that tight, wound-up feeling is still just... there. \n\nIt's like I can't fully relax. My mind always drifts back to what happened on Friday, or what I'm going to have to deal with on Monday. So, no, it doesn't really go away, even when I'm away from the building.\n\nDoctor: Beyond this situation with your manager and the procedures at work, are there other things in your life right now — like your health, finances, relationships, or other responsibilities — that are causing you to worry or feel anxious?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... everything else is actually fine. My husband and I are doing good, my health is fine, and we don't have any major money issues or anything like that. \n\nIt really is just... just work. If it weren't for this situation with the new manager and all the changes on the inspection line, I wouldn't be feeling this way at all. It's really just that one thing causing all of this.\n\nDoctor: If this situation with your manager were to be completely resolved — say he left the company or the procedures went back to how they were — do you think this wound-up, anxious feeling would go away, or do you think it might stick around even after the problem itself is solved?\n\nPatient: I really think it would go away. I mean, like I said before, this situation hasn't ended yet. I still have to go in there and report to him, and I'm still dealing with all those new procedures every single day. So I haven't had a chance to see what it would be like if it were fixed. But I really believe that if he were gone, or things went back to normal... I'd be fine. I haven't had any time where the problem was solved and I just kept feeling this way for months on my own. It's just because I'm still in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: Since this started two months ago, has the intensity of that wound-up, anxious feeling stayed pretty much the same the whole time, or has it gotten worse, or has it actually improved at all even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's stayed pretty much the same. I mean, it definitely hasn't gotten any better, because the situation at work hasn't changed at all. But I don't know that it's gotten a whole lot worse, either. \n\nIt's just... kind of a constant, steady level of being on edge. Ever since he came in and changed all the procedures a couple months ago, that feeling has just been there, pretty much at the exact same intensity the whole time.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed at night unable to sleep, or when you're feeling that wound-up sensation — are you actively thinking about work and the manager in those moments, or does the anxious feeling come up even when your mind isn't focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I know I think about work a lot when I'm lying there in bed... but I haven't really noticed if I feel that way when my mind is on other things. I guess I just haven't paid attention to whether the feeling comes up when I'm not focused on the manager or the inspection line. I just know I'm lying there feeling on edge, but I couldn't tell you for sure what exactly is going through my head every single time it happens.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask this a different way: If you were to take a full week of vacation away from work — completely away from the office, your manager, and everything related to it — do you think that wound-up, anxious feeling would go away during that time, or would it still be there with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't taken a vacation since all this started, so I haven't had a chance to see how I'd feel. I mean, I'd like to think I'd feel better if I were away from it all for a week, but honestly, I just don't know for certain if that feeling would completely go away or if it would kind of... follow me, you know? I just haven't been in that situation to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that really captures your full attention — like reading a book you can't put down, or having an engaging conversation with your husband — does that wound-up, anxious feeling fade into the background while you're focused on that activity, or does it stay with you no matter what you're doing?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm really not sure about that, either. I haven't paid close attention to whether it fades or not. \n\nIt feels like it's always there, but... I guess if I'm really focused on something else, maybe I'm just not noticing it for a minute? I don't really know. I haven't been reading much lately, and when I talk to my husband, we usually end up talking about my manager anyway. So I just couldn't say for sure if the feeling actually goes away when I'm distracted.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you noticed any days or periods where you felt that wound-up, on-edge feeling was noticeably less intense or actually absent — even if just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't kept track of it like that. It just feels like it's been a constant thing since my manager started getting on my case, but I haven't really stopped to think if there was a specific day or a few hours where it was less intense. I just know I feel wound up most of the time.\n\nDoctor: When you're away from work and something happens that completely distracts you — like if your husband made you laugh really hard, or you got unexpected good news — does that wound-up feeling actually decrease in those moments, or does it stay at the same level no matter what?\n\nPatient: I don't think I can really say. I haven't really had any unexpected good news lately, and like I said, when I'm with my husband, we're usually just talking about work. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether a laugh makes it go away for a second. It just feels like that tension is always kind of sitting there, you know? I haven't really noticed it dropping off in those kinds of moments.\n\nDoctor: I know you haven't taken a vacation since this started, but let me ask: on weekends or evenings when you're completely away from work and the manager, do you find that the wound-up feeling gradually gets better as the hours pass, or does it stay pretty much the same level of intensity throughout your time off?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that, to be honest. I haven't paid attention to whether it actually fades as the weekend goes on. It feels like I'm just always thinking about having to go back in on Monday, so I can't say for certain if the intensity drops on a Saturday night or if it just stays exactly the same. I just haven't tracked it that closely to know if it gradually gets better.\n\nDoctor: If your manager were fired tomorrow and the procedures went back to exactly how they were before, but you still had to keep working at the same job in the same building — do you think that wound-up feeling would go away, or do you think it might stay with you even though the stressor itself would be gone?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably go away. I mean, the situation hasn't ended—he's still my manager, and I still have to do things his way. So I haven't really had a chance to see what would happen if the problem was actually fixed. But I feel like if he was gone and things went back to normal, I wouldn't feel this way anymore. It's not like I've had these issues just keep going on for months after a problem was already resolved. It's really just tied to him and the changes he made.\n\nDoctor: Since this all started two months ago, have you ever had a full day or longer where you weren't thinking about work or your manager at all — and if so, did that wound-up, on-edge feeling actually go away during that time, or was it still there even when work wasn't on your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had a day like that. Work is always on my mind, even on the weekends. So I can't really say if the feeling would go away if I wasn't thinking about it, because I'm always thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that work is always on your mind, even on weekends. But I'm wondering: when you do manage to shift your attention away from work for a bit — say you're focused on a movie or a conversation — does that tight, wound-up feeling actually lessen while you're focused on something else, or does it stay just as intense regardless of what you're thinking about?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I don't think I've really noticed. Even if I'm watching a movie or talking to someone, it feels like work is just right there in the back of my head. I haven't really paid attention to whether the physical tightness lessens or not when I'm distracted. I just know I always feel tense.\n\nDoctor: I know work is always on your mind, but let me ask this: On those times when you do manage to push work out of your thoughts for a bit — maybe during a movie or a conversation — does that tight, wound-up feeling actually get better or less intense in those moments, or does it stay just as strong even when work isn't what you're actively thinking about?\n\nPatient: I think... well, I really just don't know. Like I was saying, it's hard to tell because I don't feel like I ever really push it out of my thoughts completely. Even if I'm trying to watch a movie, the stress is just sort of hovering there. So I haven't noticed if the physical tightness actually gets any better. It just feels like I'm always wound up.\n\nDoctor: When you do manage to fall asleep at night, do you sleep fairly well once you're actually asleep, or do you find yourself waking up still feeling that wound-up, tense feeling?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm not really sure. Once I finally get to sleep... I don't really know if I'm sleeping well or not. I haven't really noticed waking up in the middle of the night feeling tense, or at least I don't remember it if I do. I just know it takes a long time to actually drift off in the first place.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I don't think I've really noticed. Even if I'm watching a movie or talking to someone, it feels like work is just right there in the back of my head. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's hard to say. I haven't actually taken a week off or gone out of town in quite a while, so... I really don't have a good answer for that. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist here. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... things have just been really difficult at work lately. We got a new operations manager, and he's just been picking apart the inspection procedures I've used for years. It's like everything I do is suddenly wrong. \n\nAnd the problem is, I can't seem to leave work at work anymore. I come home just completely wound up. I end up snapping at my husband over nothing, and then I lie awake at night just replaying all these meetings in my head. It's exhausting. And then the next day, I'm second-guessing routine quality calls that I used to make without even thinking twice about it. I just don't feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of being wound up and having trouble sleeping — was it around the time this new manager arrived, or had you been feeling this way before that?\n\nPatient: I was doing completely fine before he got there. None of this was happening before the new operations manager came in. \n\nThe trouble really started building within just a few weeks of him making all those changes to our procedures. I'd say... by the second month, I knew for sure that my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. That's when the trouble sleeping and being so wound up at home really became obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these moments of feeling wound up and anxious, do they tend to happen when you're thinking about work or dealing with work-related situations, or do you find yourself feeling this way even when you're not focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely tied to work. I mean, the situation hasn't ended. I still have to report to him every day and deal with all the procedures he changed. It's not like the issue was resolved and I just kept feeling this way for months afterward for no reason. It's happening because I'm still right in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: On days when you don't have to go to work, or when you're doing something completely unrelated to this manager or the procedures — like spending time with your husband or doing something you enjoy — do you still find yourself feeling that wound-up, anxious feeling, or does it mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it doesn't completely go away on the weekends, no. I wish it did. Even when I'm just sitting on the couch with my husband trying to watch a movie, or if we go out to run errands... I might not be actively thinking about the manager right that second, but that tight, wound-up feeling is still just... there. \n\nIt's like I can't fully relax. My mind always drifts back to what happened on Friday, or what I'm going to have to deal with on Monday. So, no, it doesn't really go away, even when I'm away from the building.\n\nDoctor: Beyond this situation with your manager and the procedures at work, are there other things in your life right now — like your health, finances, relationships, or other responsibilities — that are causing you to worry or feel anxious?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... everything else is actually fine. My husband and I are doing good, my health is fine, and we don't have any major money issues or anything like that. \n\nIt really is just... just work. If it weren't for this situation with the new manager and all the changes on the inspection line, I wouldn't be feeling this way at all. It's really just that one thing causing all of this.\n\nDoctor: If this situation with your manager were to be completely resolved — say he left the company or the procedures went back to how they were — do you think this wound-up, anxious feeling would go away, or do you think it might stick around even after the problem itself is solved?\n\nPatient: I really think it would go away. I mean, like I said before, this situation hasn't ended yet. I still have to go in there and report to him, and I'm still dealing with all those new procedures every single day. So I haven't had a chance to see what it would be like if it were fixed. But I really believe that if he were gone, or things went back to normal... I'd be fine. I haven't had any time where the problem was solved and I just kept feeling this way for months on my own. It's just because I'm still in the middle of it.\n\nDoctor: Since this started two months ago, has the intensity of that wound-up, anxious feeling stayed pretty much the same the whole time, or has it gotten worse, or has it actually improved at all even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's stayed pretty much the same. I mean, it definitely hasn't gotten any better, because the situation at work hasn't changed at all. But I don't know that it's gotten a whole lot worse, either. \n\nIt's just... kind of a constant, steady level of being on edge. Ever since he came in and changed all the procedures a couple months ago, that feeling has just been there, pretty much at the exact same intensity the whole time.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed at night unable to sleep, or when you're feeling that wound-up sensation — are you actively thinking about work and the manager in those moments, or does the anxious feeling come up even when your mind isn't focused on work at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I know I think about work a lot when I'm lying there in bed... but I haven't really noticed if I feel that way when my mind is on other things. I guess I just haven't paid attention to whether the feeling comes up when I'm not focused on the manager or the inspection line. I just know I'm lying there feeling on edge, but I couldn't tell you for sure what exactly is going through my head every single time it happens.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask this a different way: If you were to take a full week of vacation away from work — completely away from the office, your manager, and everything related to it — do you think that wound-up, anxious feeling would go away during that time, or would it still be there with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't taken a vacation since all this started, so I haven't had a chance to see how I'd feel. I mean, I'd like to think I'd feel better if I were away from it all for a week, but honestly, I just don't know for certain if that feeling would completely go away or if it would kind of... follow me, you know? I just haven't been in that situation to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that really captures your full attention — like reading a book you can't put down, or having an engaging conversation with your husband — does that wound-up, anxious feeling fade into the background while you're focused on that activity, or does it stay with you no matter what you're doing?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm really not sure about that, either. I haven't paid close attention to whether it fades or not. \n\nIt feels like it's always there, but... I guess if I'm really focused on something else, maybe I'm just not noticing it for a minute? I don't really know. I haven't been reading much lately, and when I talk to my husband, we usually end up talking about my manager anyway. So I just couldn't say for sure if the feeling actually goes away when I'm distracted.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you noticed any days or periods where you felt that wound-up, on-edge feeling was noticeably less intense or actually absent — even if just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't kept track of it like that. It just feels like it's been a constant thing since my manager started getting on my case, but I haven't really stopped to think if there was a specific day or a few hours where it was less intense. I just know I feel wound up most of the time.\n\nDoctor: When you're away from work and something happens that completely distracts you — like if your husband made you laugh really hard, or you got unexpected good news — does that wound-up feeling actually decrease in those moments, or does it stay at the same level no matter what?\n\nPatient: I don't think I can really say. I haven't really had any unexpected good news lately, and like I said, when I'm with my husband, we're usually just talking about work. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether a laugh makes it go away for a second. It just feels like that tension is always kind of sitting there, you know? I haven't really noticed it dropping off in those kinds of moments.\n\nDoctor: I know you haven't taken a vacation since this started, but let me ask: on weekends or evenings when you're completely away from work and the manager, do you find that the wound-up feeling gradually gets better as the hours pass, or does it stay pretty much the same level of intensity throughout your time off?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that, to be honest. I haven't paid attention to whether it actually fades as the weekend goes on. It feels like I'm just always thinking about having to go back in on Monday, so I can't say for certain if the intensity drops on a Saturday night or if it just stays exactly the same. I just haven't tracked it that closely to know if it gradually gets better.\n\nDoctor: If your manager were fired tomorrow and the procedures went back to exactly how they were before, but you still had to keep working at the same job in the same building — do you think that wound-up feeling would go away, or do you think it might stay with you even though the stressor itself would be gone?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably go away. I mean, the situation hasn't ended—he's still my manager, and I still have to do things his way. So I haven't really had a chance to see what would happen if the problem was actually fixed. But I feel like if he was gone and things went back to normal, I wouldn't feel this way anymore. It's not like I've had these issues just keep going on for months after a problem was already resolved. It's really just tied to him and the changes he made.\n\nDoctor: Since this all started two months ago, have you ever had a full day or longer where you weren't thinking about work or your manager at all — and if so, did that wound-up, on-edge feeling actually go away during that time, or was it still there even when work wasn't on your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had a day like that. Work is always on my mind, even on the weekends. So I can't really say if the feeling would go away if I wasn't thinking about it, because I'm always thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that work is always on your mind, even on weekends. But I'm wondering: when you do manage to shift your attention away from work for a bit — say you're focused on a movie or a conversation — does that tight, wound-up feeling actually lessen while you're focused on something else, or does it stay just as intense regardless of what you're thinking about?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly.     "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F41.1\", \"F43.21\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist here.
